Enumerating independent linear inferences \rsuper * Thanks: The authors would like to thank Matteo Acclavio, Ross Horne, Lutz Straßburger, and Alessio Guglielmi for several comments and discussions on this and earlier versions of this work.
Anupam Das ORCID:0000-0002-0142-3676, Alex Rice ORCID:0000-0002-2698-5122

Abstract
. A linear inference is a valid inequality of Boolean algebra in which each variable occurs at most once on each side. In this work we leverage recently developed graphical representations of linear formulae to build an implementation that is capable of more efficiently searching for switch-medial-independent inferences. We use it to find four ‘minimal’ 8-variable independent inferences and also prove that no smaller ones exist; in contrast, a previous approach based directly on formulae reached computational limits already at 7 variables. Two of these new inferences derive some previously found independent linear inferences. The other two (which are dual) exhibit structure seemingly beyond the scope of previous approaches we are aware of; in particular, their existence contradicts a conjecture of Das and Straßburger. We were also able to identify 10 minimal 9-variable linear inferences independent of all the aforementioned inferences, comprising 5 dual pairs, and present applications of our implementation to recent ‘graph logics’.



Nov. 10, 2021May 19, 2023

, published in the proceedings of FSCD’21.

1.
Introduction

A
linear inference
is a valid implication
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
of Boolean logic, where
\varphi
and
\psi
are
linear
, i.e. each variable occurs at most once in each of
\varphi
and
\psi
.
Such implications have played a crucial role in many areas of structural proof theory.
For instance the inference
switch
,

\mathsf{s}\ :\ x\land(y\lor z)\ \rightarrow\ (x\land y)\lor z

governs the logical behaviour of the
multiplicative
connectives
\invamp
and
\otimes
of linear logic
[
Gir87
]
,
and similarly the inference
medial
,

\mathsf{m}\ :\ (w\land x)\lor(y\land z)\ \rightarrow\ (w\lor y)\land(x\lor z)

together with the structural rules
weakening
and
contraction
, governs the logical behaviour of the
additive
connectives
\oplus
and
\&

[
Str02
,
Str03
]
.
Both of these inferences are fundamental to
deep inference
proof theory, in particular allowing weakening and contraction to be reduced to atomic form
[
BT01
,
Brü03a
]
, thereby admitting elegant ‘geometric’ proof normalisation procedures based on
atomic flows

[
GG08
,
Gun09
]
.
One particular feature of these normalisation procedures is that they are robust under the addition of further linear inferences to the system, thanks to the atomisation of structural steps.

On the other hand the set of
all
linear inferences
L
plays an essential role in certain models of linear logic and related substructural logics.
In particular, the multiplicative fragment of Blass’
game semantics
model of linear logic validates
just
the linear inferences (there called ‘binary tautologies’)
[
Bla92
]
, and this coincides too with the multiplicative fragment of Japaridze’s
computability logic
, cf., e.g.,
[
Jap05
]
.
From a complexity theoretic point of view, the set
L
is sufficiently rich to encode all of Boolean logic: it is
\mathbf{co}\mathbf{NP}
-complete
[
Str12b
,
DS16
]
.

It was recently shown by one of the authors, together with Straßburger, that, despite its significance,
L
admits no polynomial-time axiomatisation by linear inferences unless
\mathbf{co}\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{NP}

[
DS15
,
DS16
]
, resolving a long-standing open problem of Blass and Japaridze for their respective logics (see, e.g.,
[
Jap17
]
).
Note that the condition of polynomial-time computability naturally arises from proof theory
[
CR74
,
CR79
]
, and is also required for the result to be meaningful: it prevents us just taking the entire set
L
as an axiomatisation.

From a Boolean algebra point of view, this result means that the class of linear Boolean inequalities has no feasible basis (unless
\mathbf{co}\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{NP}
).
From a proof theoretic point of view this means that any propositional proof system (in the Cook-Reckhow sense
[
CR74
,
CR79
]
, see also
[
Kra19
]
) must necessarily admit some ‘structural’ behaviour, even when restricted to proving only linear inferences (unless
\mathbf{co}\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{NP}
).

An immediate consequence of this result is that
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{m}
above do not suffice to generate all linear inferences (unless
\mathbf{co}\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{NP}
), even modulo all valid linear equations (which are just associativity, commutativity, and unit laws, cf.
[
DS15
,
DS16
]
).
In fact, this was known before the aforementioned result, due to the identification of an explicit 36 variable inference in
[
Str12b
]
.
1
1

1

Straßburger refers to the inference as a ‘balanced tautology’, but like the ‘binary tautologies’ of Blass and Japaridze, these are equivalent to linear inferences. In particular we recast Straßburger’s example as a bona fide linear inference in
Section

3.1
.

Already in that work the question was posed whether such an inference was minimal, and since then the identification of a minimal
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inference has been a recurring theme in the literature of this area.

It has been verified in
[
Das13
]
that a minimal
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inference must be ‘non-trivial’, as long as we admit all valid linear equations (again, these are just associativity, commutativity and unit laws).
Intuitively, ‘non-triviality’ rules out pathological inferences such as
x\land y\rightarrow x\lor y
or
x\land(y\lor z)\rightarrow x\lor(y\land z)
. For these inferences the variable, say,
y
is, in a sense, redundant; it turns out that they may be derived in
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
, modulo linear equations, from a smaller non-trivial ‘core’.
We recall these arguments in
Section

2
.

Furthermore
[
Das13
]
identified a 10 variable linear inference that is not derivable by switch and medial (even under linear equations), which Straßburger conjectured was minimal
[
Str12a
]
.
Around the same time Šipraga attempted a computational approach, searching for independent linear inferences by brute force
[
Š12
]
.
However, computational limits were reached already at 7 variables.
In particular, every linear inference of up to 6 variables is already derivable by switch and medial, modulo linear equations; due to the aforementioned 10 variable inference, any minimal independent linear inference must have size 7,8,9, or 10.

Since 2013 there have been significant advances in the area, in particular through the proliferation of
graph-theoretic
tools.
Indeed, the interplay between formulae and graphs was heavily exploited for the aforementioned result of
[
DS15
,
DS16
]
.
Since then, multiple works have emerged in the world of linear proof theory that treat these graphs as ‘first class citizens’, comprising a now active area of research
[
NS18
,
AHS20
,
AHS22
,
CDW20
]
.

Contribution

In this work we revisit the question of minimal
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inferences by exploiting the aforementioned recent graph theoretic techniques.
Such an approach vastly reduces the computational resources necessary and, in particular, we are able to provide a conclusive result:
the smallest
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inference has size 8. In fact there are four minimal (w.r.t. derivability) such ones, unique up to associativity, commutativity, renaming of variables, given by,

\displaystyle(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))

(1)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(z\land(x\lor y))\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land((w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))

(2)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle((z\lor(w\land x))\land(x^{\prime}\lor y))\lor(z^{\prime}\land(w^{\prime}\lor y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle((w\land w^{\prime})\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))\land((y\land y^{\prime})\lor(z\land z^{\prime}))

(3)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(w\land y)\lor((x\lor(w^{\prime}\land z^{\prime}))\land((x^{\prime}\land y^{\prime})\lor z))

and the De Morgan dual of
Eq.

3
.
Note that
Eq.

2
did not appear in the preliminary version
[
DR21
]
as it was erroneously considered to be isomorphic to the first.
We dedicate some discussion to each of these separately in
Section

3.2
, and include a manual verification of their soundness and
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence in
Appendix

A
, as a sanity check.

Our main contribution, as announced in the preliminary version of this paper
[
DR21
]
, is an implementation that checks inference for
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivability, which was able to confirm that all 7 variable linear inference are derivable from switch and medial. In fact we found (
1
) independently of the implementation presented in this paper.
2
2

2

These two developments were respectively communicated via blog posts
[
Ric20
]
and
[
Das20
]
.

Ultimately, we improved the implementation to run on inferences of size 8 too,
and our inference (
1
) was duly found, as well as (
2
), (
3
), and the dual of (
3
).
One highlight of (
3
) is that it exhibits a peculiar structural property that
refutes
Conjecture 7.9 from
[
DS16
]
, as we explain in
Section

3.2.2
.

In this extended version, we also show how our search algorithm can be generalised to enumerate minimal linear inferences independent of an arbitrary set
S
of linear inferences.
This can be employed to devise a recursive algorithm
\mathsf{basis}(n)
that computes all
n
-variable minimal linear inferences independent of all smaller ones.
To this end, in
Section

6
we define the notion of an
n
-minimal set, which is intuitively a minimal set of inferences that derives all
n
-variable inferences modulo all
<n
-variable inferences.
We show these
n
-minimal sets are unique up to linear equations and renaming of variables, and finally prove correctness of our algorithm
\mathsf{basis}(n)
by extending some theoretical results on constant-freeness for switch and medial to the general setting of arbitrary
n
-minimal sets.
For instance the 9-variable minimal linear inferences given by
\mathsf{basis}(9)
are those not derivable from switch, medial, and the four aforementioned 8-variable linear inferences, instead of the weaker condition of not being derivable from only switch and medial.
Indeed, we were able to successfully run
\mathsf{basis}(9)
to compute the
9
-minimal linear inferences.
We classify these in
Section

7
, identifying 10 such inferences in total, comprising 5 dual pairs.

Our implementation
[
Ric21
]
is split into a
library
and an
executable
, where the executable implements our search algorithm described in
Section

5.2
, and the library contains foundations for working with linear inferences using the graph theoretic techniques presented in
Section

4
. These are written in Rust and designed to be relatively fast while maintaining readability.

In addition to having the machinery needed for our original search, the library has also been extended for to work with
arbitrary graphs
, e.g. as presented in
[
CDW20
]
, in particular being able to check other notions of inference for graphs and having tools for determining whether an inference is an instance of an arbitrary, user-supplied graph rewrite.
Our intention is that this could form a reusable base for future investigations in the area, both for linear formulae and for the recent linear graph theoretic settings of
[
NS18
,
AHS20
,
AHS22
,
CDW20
]
.

As well as the additions already mentioned, this extended version contains several further proofs, examples and general discussion compared with the preliminary version
[
DR21
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we present some preliminaries on linear inferences, triviality and minimality.
The content of this section is based on Section 2 of the preliminary version,
[
DR21
]
, with some additional proofs and narrative.

Throughout this paper we shall work with a countably infinite set of
variables
, written
x,y,z
etc.
A
linear formula
on a (finite) set of variables
\mathcal{V}
is defined recursively as follows:

•

\top
and
\bot
are linear formulae on
\varnothing
, the empty set of variables (called
units
or
constants
).

•

x
and
\neg x
are linear formulae on
\{x\}
, for each variable
x
.

•

If
\varphi
is a linear formula on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\psi
is a linear formula on
\mathcal{V}_{2}
, with
\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}=\varnothing
, then
\varphi\lor\psi
and
\varphi\land\psi
are linear formulae on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\cup\mathcal{V}_{2}
.

We adopt the expected Boolean semantics of (linear) formulae. Namely we extend any map
\alpha
from variables to
\{0,1\}
(an ‘
assignment
’) to one on all (linear) formulae as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ := \ }l}\alpha(\top)&1\\
\alpha(\bot)&0\\
\alpha(\neg x)&1-\alpha(x)\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{r@{\ := \ }l}\alpha(\varphi\land\psi)&\min(\alpha(\varphi),\alpha(\psi))\\
\alpha(\varphi\lor\psi)&\max(\alpha(\varphi),\alpha(\psi))\end{array}

The formula
\varphi

satisfies
an assignment
\alpha
if
\alpha(\varphi)=1
.
Formulae
\varphi
and
\psi
are
logically equivalent
if they satisfy the same assignments.

Remark 1

(On negation)
.

Note that our restriction of negation to only propositional variables is made without loss of generality. Namely, we may recover
\neg\varphi
thanks to De Morgan’s laws by setting:

\neg\bot:=\top\qquad\neg\top:=\bot\qquad\neg\neg\varphi:=\varphi\qquad\neg(\varphi\lor\psi):=\neg\varphi\land\neg\psi\qquad\neg(\varphi\land\psi):=\neg\varphi\lor\neg\psi

Note in particular that, if
\varphi
is a linear formula on
\mathcal{V}
, then so is
\neg\varphi
.

A linear formula that does not contain
\top
or
\bot
is
constant-free
.
A linear formula with no negated variables (i.e. formulae of form
\neg x
) is
negation-free
. Later in the paper, we will be able to restrict our search to inferences between constant-free negation-free formulae.

In what follows, we shall omit explicit consideration of variable sets, assuming that they are disjoint whenever required by the notation being used.

A binary relation
\sim
on linear formulae is
closed under contexts
if we have for all
\varphi,\psi
, and
\chi
:

\displaystyle\varphi\sim\psi\implies\varphi\land\chi\sim\psi\land\chi

\displaystyle\varphi\sim\psi\implies\varphi\lor\chi\sim\psi\lor\chi

\displaystyle\varphi\sim\psi\implies\chi\land\varphi\sim\chi\land\psi

\displaystyle\varphi\sim\psi\implies\chi\lor\varphi\sim\chi\lor\psi

An equivalence relation (on linear formulae) that is closed under contexts is called a (linear)
congruence
.

{defi}

[Linear equations]
Let
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
be the smallest congruence satisfying,

\displaystyle\varphi\lor\psi\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi\lor\varphi

\displaystyle\varphi\land(\psi\land\chi)\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}(\varphi\land\psi)\land\chi

\displaystyle\varphi\land\psi\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi\land\varphi

\displaystyle\varphi\lor(\psi\lor\chi)\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}(\varphi\lor\psi)\lor\chi

\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
is the smallest congruence satisfying:

\displaystyle\varphi\land\top\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\varphi

\displaystyle\varphi\lor\bot\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\varphi

\displaystyle\top\land\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\varphi

\displaystyle\bot\lor\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\varphi

(4)

\displaystyle\varphi\land\bot\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\bot

\displaystyle\varphi\lor\top\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\top

\displaystyle\bot\land\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\bot

\displaystyle\top\lor\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{u}}\top

\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
is the smallest congruence containing both
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
and
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
.

Remark 2

(Variable sets under congruence)
.

Suppose
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are linear formulae on
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
respectively.
If
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\varphi^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Note also that
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
generates a unique normal form of linear formulae by maximally eliminating constants:

Proposition 3

(Folklore)
.

Every formula is
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
-equivalent to a unique constant-free formula, or is equivalent to
\bot
or
\top
.

Proof 2.1
.

Construe the equations of
Eq.

4
as a rewrite system by orienting them left-to-right.
This system reduces the size of formulae and so must terminate.
By inspection, the normal form must contain no constant under the scope of a connective
\lor
or
\land
.

For uniqueness, it suffices to consider the critical pairs. In fact, the analysis is particularly simple since any critical pair is a
\lor
or
\land
of constants that always reduces to the same constant:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\top&\underset{\top\land}{\longleftarrow}&\top\land\top&\underset{\land\top}{\longrightarrow}&\top\\
\bot&\underset{\top\land}{\longleftarrow}&\top\land\bot&\underset{\land\bot}{\longrightarrow}&\bot\\
\bot&\underset{\bot\land}{\longleftarrow}&\bot\land\top&\underset{\land\top}{\longrightarrow}&\bot\\
\bot&\underset{\bot\land}{\longleftarrow}&\bot\land\bot&\underset{\land\bot}{\longrightarrow}&\bot\end{array}\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\top&\underset{\top\lor}{\longleftarrow}&\top\lor\top&\underset{\lor\top}{\longrightarrow}&\top\\
\top&\underset{\top\lor}{\longleftarrow}&\top\lor\bot&\underset{\lor\bot}{\longrightarrow}&\top\\
\top&\underset{\bot\lor}{\longleftarrow}&\bot\lor\top&\underset{\lor\top}{\longrightarrow}&\top\\
\bot&\underset{\bot\lor}{\longleftarrow}&\bot\lor\bot&\underset{\lor\bot}{\longrightarrow}&\bot\end{array}

For the above pairs, we have indicated a reduction, say
\top\land\varphi\to\varphi
or
\varphi\lor\top\to\top
, by
\top\land
or
\lor\top
respectively.

Remark 4

(On logical equivalence)
.

Clearly, if
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\psi
then
\varphi
and
\psi
are logically equivalent.
In fact, for linear formulae, we also have a converse: two linear formulae
\varphi
and
\psi
on the same variables are logically equivalent if and only if
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\psi

[
DS15
,
DS16
]
. Furthermore, given two linear formulae
\varphi
and
\psi
that are constant-free,
\bot
, or
\top
, then
\varphi
and
\psi
are logically equivalent if and only if they are
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
-equivalent.
These property follows from
3
above, the results of
Section

2.2
, and the graphical representation of linear formulae and their semantics in
Section

4
.

2.1.
Linear inferences

A
linear inference

is just a valid implication
\varphi\to\psi
(i.e.,
\alpha(\varphi)=1\implies\alpha(\psi)=1
) where
\varphi
and
\psi
are linear formulae.
The left-hand side (LHS) and right-hand side (RHS) of a linear inference, generally speaking, need not be linear formulae on the same variables.
Nonetheless we shall occasionally refer to linear inferences “on
\mathcal{V}
” or “on
n
variables”, assuming that the LHS and RHS are both linear formulae on some fixed
\mathcal{V}
with
|\mathcal{V}|=n
.

There are two linear inferences we shall particularly focus on, due to their prevalence in structural proof theory.
Switch
is the following inference on 3 variables,

\mathsf{s}:x\land(y\lor z)\to(x\land y)\lor z

(5)

and
medial
is the following inference on 4 variables:

\mathsf{m}:(w\land x)\lor(y\land z)\to(w\lor y)\land(x\lor z)

(6)

We may compose switch and medial (and more generally an arbitrary set of linear inferences) to form new linear inferences by construing them as
term rewriting rules
.
More generally, we will consider rewriting derivations modulo the equivalence relations
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
and
\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
we introduced earlier.
In the latter case, as previously mentioned, the underlying set of variables may change during a derivation, though
3
will later allow us to work with some fixed set of variables throughout
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
derivations.

{defi}

[Rewriting]
Let
S
be a set of linear inferences.
We write
\to_{S}
for the term rewrite system generated by
S
. I.e.,
\to_{S}
is the smallest relation containing each inference of
S
and closed under substitution and contexts.

We write
\rightsquigarrow_{S}
for the closure of
\to_{S}
modulo
\mathsf{ac}
, i.e.
\varphi\rightsquigarrow_{S}\psi
if there are
\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}
such that
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\varphi^{\prime}\to_{S}\psi^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi
. In this case we may say that the inference
\varphi\to\psi
is an
instance (without units)
of some inference in
S
.
We write
\rightsquigarrow_{S\mathsf{u}}
for the closure of
\to_{S}
modulo
\mathsf{acu}
, i.e.
\varphi\rightsquigarrow_{S\mathsf{u}}\psi
if there are
\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}
such that
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\varphi^{\prime}\to_{S}\psi^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\psi
. Similarly in this case we may simply say the inference is an
instance (with units)
of some inference in
S
.

We write
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{S}
, or
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{S\mathsf{u}}
, for the smallest reflexive transitive relation containing
\rightsquigarrow_{S}
and
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
, or
\rightsquigarrow_{S\mathsf{u}}
and
\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
, respectively, and say that a linear inference
\varphi\to\psi
is
S
-derivable (without units)
(or
S
-derivable with units
) if
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{S}\psi
(or
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{X\mathsf{u}}\psi
, respectively).

We shall typically write such sets
S
by just listing their linear inferences, e.g. we write
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{ms}}
instead of
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{s}\}}
and
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}
instead of
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\{\mathsf{m},\mathsf{s}\}\mathsf{u}}
, in order to lighten notation.
Clearly,
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{m}
are valid, so any derivation
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\psi
comprises a linear inference.

{exa}

[Weakening and duality]

The following derives
\bot\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\chi
for any
\chi
:

\bot\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\bot\land(\top\lor\chi)\to_{\mathsf{s}}(\bot\land\top)\lor\chi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\chi

Using this, we may
\{\mathsf{s}\}
-derive
weakening
,
\varphi\to\varphi\lor\chi
, with units as follows:

\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\varphi\lor\bot\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\varphi\lor\varphi\lor\chi

Notice that
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}
is closed under De Morgan duality:
If
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\chi
and
\bar{\varphi}
and
\bar{\chi}
are obtained from
\varphi
and
\chi
, respectively, by flipping each
\lor
to a
\land
and vice versa, then
\bar{\chi}\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\bar{\varphi}
.
This follows by direct inspection of
\mathsf{s}
and each clause of
\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
; indeed the same property holds for
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}
,
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{ms}}
, and
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}
by the same reasoning.
As a result, we also have that
coweakening
,
\varphi\land\chi\to\varphi
, is
\{\mathsf{s}\}
-derivable with units.

{exa}

[‘Mix’]

Units can help us derive even constant-free linear inferences.
For instance,
mix
:
\varphi\land\psi\to\varphi\lor\psi
can be derived as a composite of coweakening and weakening:

\varphi\land\chi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}\mathsf{u}}\varphi\lor\chi

Note that mix is not derivable without using instances of
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
.

The following is one of the main results of this paper:

Theorem 5
.

Suppose
\varphi
is a linear formula over
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\psi
is a linear formula over
\mathcal{V}_{2}
and
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
is a linear inference. Then if
|\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}|\leq 7
we have that
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\psi
.

Furthermore, there is a valid linear inference
\varphi\to\psi
on
8
variables with
\varphi\not\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\psi
, so
7
is maximal with the property above.

This theorem was the original objective of the preliminary version of this paper
[
DR21
]
. Indeed, before this it was not known if the smallest linear inference independent of switch and medial used 7 or 8 variables. The rest of
Section

2
and
Sections

4
and
5
work towards a proof of this theorem.

2.2.
Trivial inferences

In order to state
5
above in its most general form, we have allowed linear formulae to include constants and negation, and linear inferences to be between formulae with different variable sets.
However
it turns out that we may proceed to prove
5
, without loss of generality, by working with derivations of only constant-free, negation-free formulae on some fixed set of variables, as was already shown in
[
Das13
]
. This is done by defining the notion of a
trivial
inference, whose
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivability, with units, may be reduced to that of a smaller non-trivial inference.

{defi}

An inference
\varphi\to\psi
is
trivial at a variable
x
if
\varphi[\top/x]\to\psi[\bot/x]
is again a valid inference.
An inference is
trivial
if it is trivial at one of its variables.

{exa}

The mix inference from
Section

2.1
,
x\land y\to x\lor y
, is trivial at
x
and trivial at
y
.
Note, however, that it is not trivial at
x
and
y
‘at the same time’, in the sense that the simultaneous substition of
\bot
for
x
and
y
in the LHS and
\top
for
x
and
y
in the RHS does not result in a valid implication.
In contrast, the linear inference
w\land(x\lor y)\to w\lor(x\land y)
from
[
Das13
]
is, indeed, trivial at
x
and
y
‘at the same time’.

Neither switch nor medial are trivial.

Remark 6

(Global vs local triviality)
.

Note that triviality is closed under composition by linear inferences: if
\varphi\to\psi
is trivial at
x
and
\psi\to\chi
is valid, then
\varphi\to\chi
is trivial at
x
.
Similarly for
\chi\to\psi
if
\chi\to\varphi
is valid.
One pertinent feature is that the converse does not hold: there are ‘globally’ trivial derivations that are nowhere ‘locally’ trivial.
For instance consider the following derivation (from
[
DS16
, Remark 5.6]
):

w\land x\land(y\lor z)\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}w\land((x\land y)\lor z)\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}(w\land z)\lor(x\land y)\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{m}}(w\lor x)\land(y\lor z)

The derived inference is just an instance of mix, from
Section

2.1
, on the redex
w\land x
, which is trivial.
However, no local step is trivial.

To prove (the first half of)
5
, in
Section

5
we will actually prove the following apparent weakening of that statement:

Theorem 7
.

Let
n<8
. Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be constant-free negation-free linear formulae on
n
variables and suppose
\varphi\to\psi
is a non-trivial linear inference. Then
\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
.

In fact this statement is no weaker at all, and we will now see how the consideration of triviality allows us to only deal with such special cases without loss of generality. We first show that is not necessary to consider linear inferences where the premise and conclusion have different variables.

Proposition 8
.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be linear formulae on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
, respectively, and let
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
be a linear inference. Then there is
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}:\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}
such that
\mathsf{r}
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\}
-derivable with units.

Proof 2.2
.

First note that by setting
\varphi=\top
in
Section

2.1
we have for any
\psi
:

\psi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\top\land\psi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\top\lor\psi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\top

and by setting
\psi=\bot
we have for all
\varphi
that:

\bot\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\varphi\land\bot\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\varphi\lor\bot\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\varphi

Now we can let
\varphi^{\prime}
be
\varphi
with every variable in
\mathcal{V}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{2}
(or its negation) replaced with
\top
, and
\psi^{\prime}
be
\psi
with every variable in
\mathcal{V}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{1}
(or its negation) replaced with
\bot
. Then
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}:\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
is clearly a linear inference (since
\mathsf{r}
is valid w.r.t. all assignments) and only uses the variables
\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}
. Furthermore we have the derivation:

\varphi\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\varphi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{r}^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\psi

where the first step applies the derivation of
x\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\top
(or
\neg x\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\top
) to each variable
x\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{2}
and the last step applies the derivation of
\bot\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}y
(or
\bot\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{msu}}\neg y
) to each variable
y\in\mathcal{V}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{1}
.

The key use of triviality is that if an inference is trivial, then there is a smaller non-trivial inference from which it can be derived.

{propC}

[
[
Das13
, Theorem 34]
]

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be linear formulae on
\mathcal{V}
,
and let
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
be a trivial linear inference.
Then there is a non-trivial linear inference
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}:\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
on some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\}
-derivable with units.

Note in particular that, in both statements above, if
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable with units, then so is
\mathsf{r}
.
This is also the case for the next result.

Proposition 9
.

Let
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
be a non-trivial linear inference among variables
\mathcal{V}\neq\varnothing
.
Then there is a constant-free negation-free non-trivial linear inference
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}:\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{V}

s.t.
\mathsf{r}:\varphi\to\psi
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\}
-derivable with units.

Proof 2.3
.

First, note that both
\varphi
and
\psi
must be linear formulae on
\mathcal{V}
, since
\varphi\to\psi
is non-trivial.
For the same reason, no variable can occur positively in
\varphi
and negatively in
\psi
or vice-versa, since
\varphi\to\psi
is non-trivial, and so any negated variable may be safely replaced by its positive counterpart.
From here, we simply set
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
to be the constant-free formulae (uniquely) obtained from
3
by
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
.
Non-triviality of
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathsf{r}
by logical equivalence.

Corollary 10
.

The statement of
7
implies (the first half of) the statement of
5
.

Proof 2.4
.

Let
\mathsf{r}
be as in
5
.
Let
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
be the inference where the LHS and RHS have the same variable set obtained from
8
, let
\mathsf{r}^{\prime\prime}
be the non-trivial linear inference obtained by
Section

2.2
above, and finally let
\mathsf{r}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the non-trivial constant-free negation-free linear inference obtained by
9
.
By
7
,
\mathsf{r}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable and so, by
8
,
Section

2.2
, and
9
,
\mathsf{r}
is also
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable with units.

It is clear that if an inference is derivable with switch and medial then it is also derivable with switch, medial, and units. The following proposition, while not necessary for the proof of
10
, allows the the converse in some cases, and is the reason why our search algorithm in
Section

5
will only check for
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivability.

Proposition 11

(Follows from
[
Das13
]
, Lemma 28)
.

Suppose
\varphi\to\psi
is a non-trivial constant-free negation-free linear inference that is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable with units. Then
\varphi\to\psi
is also
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable (without units).

Proof 2.5
.

Suppose we have the following derivation of
\varphi\to\psi
, a non-trivial constant-free negation-free linear inference:

\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\varphi_{0}\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi_{0}\dots\varphi_{n}\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi_{n}\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}\psi

(7)

Using
3
, we can reduce any formula
\varphi
to a
\sim_{\mathsf{u}}
equivalent constant-free formula (or
\bot
or
\top
), which we will denote
\varphi^{\prime}
.
We can now reduce each
\varphi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
to
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}
or
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
respectively.
Now each
\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
can be replaced by a
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
by
4
as all the involved formulae are constant-free or
\bot
or
\top
.
It remains to show that each rewrite is still valid.

We prove by induction on the definition of
\to_{\mathsf{ms}}
(namely as the closure of
\mathsf{ms}
under contexts and substitution) that if
\varphi\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
then either
\varphi^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi^{\prime}
or
\varphi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi^{\prime}
.

•

Suppose we have an instance of the form
\varphi\land\chi\to\psi\land\chi
, with
\varphi\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
.

–

If
\chi^{\prime}=\bot
then indeed
(\varphi\land\chi)^{\prime}=(\psi\land\chi)^{\prime}=\bot
.

–

If
\chi^{\prime}=\top
then
(\varphi\land\chi)^{\prime}=\varphi^{\prime}
and
(\psi\land\chi)^{\prime}=\psi^{\prime}
, whence we conclude by the inductive hypothesis for
\varphi\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
.

–

Otherwise we have
\chi^{\prime}
is not a constant, and so
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
must also not be constants, or the inference would be trivial. Therefore,
(\varphi\land\chi)^{\prime}=\varphi^{\prime}\land\chi^{\prime}
and
(\psi\land\chi)^{\prime}=\psi^{\prime}\land\chi^{\prime}
.
By the inductive hypothesis for
\varphi\to\psi
we have
\varphi^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi^{\prime}
or
\varphi^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi^{\prime}
, whence we conclude by context closure of
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
and
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}
.

•

The other cases for closure under contexts follow similarly.

•

Now suppose
\varphi\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
with
\varphi=\alpha(\chi_{0},\dots,\chi_{n})
and
\psi=\beta(\chi_{0},\dots,\chi_{n})
where the inference
\alpha(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\to\beta(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
(all variables displayed) is switch or medial (and so
n<4
).

–

If each
\chi_{i}^{\prime}
is not a constant
\bot
or
\top
, then also
\varphi^{\prime}=\alpha(\chi_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{n}^{\prime})
and
\psi^{\prime}=\beta(\chi_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{n}^{\prime})
so
\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
is again an instance of switch or medial, respectively.

–

Otherwise,
\varphi^{\prime}\to\psi^{\prime}
is an instance of some (non-trivial) inference
\mathsf{r}
on
<4
variables.
However any such
\mathsf{r}
is either an instance of
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
or
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}
. (This can be checked by hand or using our computer implementation presented in
Section

5
).

Now, returning to
Eq.

7
, we have by the above argument that each
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi_{i}^{\prime}
or
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi_{i}^{\prime}
.
Since
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\ =\ \sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\cdot\to_{\mathsf{ms}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
, we can locally merge any redundant
\mathsf{ac}
steps to obtain that, indeed
\varphi^{\prime}\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi^{\prime}
.

2.3.
Minimality of inferences

Let us take a moment to explain the various notions of ‘inference minimality’ that we shall mention in this work.

Size minimality
refers simply to the number of variables the inference contains. E.g. when we say that the
8
-variable inferences in the next section are size minimal (or ‘smallest’) non-
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivable with units (or
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent
) linear inferences, we mean that there are no
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inferences with fewer variables.

A linear inference
\varphi\to\psi
is
logically minimal
if there is no logically distinct interpolating linear formula.
I.e. if
\varphi\to\chi
and
\chi\to\psi
are linear inferences, then
\chi
is logically equivalent to
\varphi
or
\psi
(and so, by
4
, is
\sim_{\mathsf{acu}}
-equivalent to
\varphi
or
\psi
).

Finally, a linear inference
\varphi\to\psi
is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimal
if there is no formula
\chi
s.t.
\varphi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\chi
or
\chi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{ms}}\psi
and
\chi\to\psi
or
\varphi\to\chi
, respectively, is a valid linear inference which is not a logical equivalence.

It is clear from the definitions that any logically minimal inference is also
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimal, though the converse may not be true.
The reason for considering
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality is that it is easier to systematically check by hand.
In fact, the implementation we give later in
Section

5
further verifies that our new
8
-variable inferences are logically minimal.

Logical minimality also serves an important purpose for our proof of
7
, as it allows the following reduction, greatly reducing the search space for our implementation, in fact to nearly
1\%
of its original size for
8
variable inferences:
3
3

3

Reduces from
514486
inferences to
5364
and
\nicefrac{{5364}}{{514486}}\approx 1.04\%
.

Lemma 12
.

Suppose the statement of
7
holds whenever
\varphi\to\psi
is logically minimal. Then the statement of
7
holds (even when
\varphi\to\psi
is not logically minimal).

Proof 2.6
.

Suppose we have a non-trivial inference between constant-free negation-free linear inferences
\varphi\to\psi
. Then
\varphi\to\psi
can be refined into a chain of logically minimal linear inferences
\varphi\to\chi_{0}\to\dots\to\chi_{n}\to\psi
. All of these must be non-trivial, as triviality of any of them would imply triviality of
\varphi\to\psi
, cf.
6
. Therefore if all such inferences are derivable from switch and medial (with units) then so is
\varphi\to\psi
, by transitivity.

3.
New 8-variable
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inferences

In this section we shall present the new 8-variable linear inferences of this work ((
1
), (
2
), and (
3
) from the introduction), and give self-contained arguments for their
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence and
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality, as a sort of sanity check for the implementation described in the next section.
We shall also briefly discuss some of their structural properties, in reference to previous works in the area.
Thanks to the results of the previous section, in particular
9
and
6
, we shall only consider non-trivial constant-free negation-free linear inferences with the same variables in the LHS and RHS.
Furthermore, by
11
we shall only consider
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-derivability (i.e. without units).

The content of this section is based on Section 3 of the preliminary version,
[
DR21
]
, with the addition of the inference (
2
) and surrounding discussion that was previously overlooked.

3.1.
Previous linear inferences

In
[
Str12b
]
Straßburger presented a 36-variable inference that is
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent, by an encoding of the pigeonhole principle with 4 pigeons and 3 holes.
He there referred to it as a ‘balanced’ tautology, but in our setting it is a linear inference that can be written as follows:
4
4

4

We write Straßburger’s inference by encoding each
q_{i1j}
as
x_{ij}
, each
q_{i2j}
as
y_{ij}
, each
q_{i3j}
as
z_{ij}
, and using ‘primed’ variables instead of duals, with the LHS of the inference being the appropriate instances of excluded middle.

\begin{array}[]{rc}&\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{3}\bigwedge\limits_{j=1}^{i}(x_{ij}\lor x_{ij}^{\prime})\land\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{3}\bigwedge\limits_{j=1}^{i}(y_{ij}\lor y_{ij}^{\prime})\land\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{3}\bigwedge\limits_{j=1}^{i}(z_{ij}\lor z_{ij}^{\prime})\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\to&\left[\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad( (}l@{)\ \land\ (}l@{)\ \land\ (}l@{))}}&x_{11}\lor x_{21}\lor x_{31}&y_{11}\lor y_{21}\lor y_{31}&z_{11}\lor z_{21}\lor z_{31}\\
\lor&x_{11}^{\prime}\lor x_{22}\lor x_{32}&y_{11}^{\prime}\lor y_{22}\lor y_{32}&z_{11}^{\prime}\lor z_{22}\lor z_{32}\\
\lor&x_{21}^{\prime}\lor x_{22}^{\prime}\lor x_{33}&y_{21}^{\prime}\lor y_{22}^{\prime}\lor y_{33}&z_{21}^{\prime}\lor z_{22}^{\prime}\lor z_{33}\\
\lor&x_{31}^{\prime}\lor x_{32}^{\prime}\lor x_{33}^{\prime}&y_{31}^{\prime}\lor y_{32}^{\prime}\lor y_{33}^{\prime}&z_{31}^{\prime}\lor z_{32}^{\prime}\lor z_{33}^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\end{array}

In
[
Das13
]
Das noticed that a more succinct encoding of the pigeonhole principle could be carried out, with only 3 pigeons and 2 holes, resulting in a 10-variable
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inference.
A variation of that, e.g. as used in
[
Das17
]
, is the following:

\displaystyle(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land(y\lor y^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})

(8)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(z\land(x\lor y))\lor(u\land x^{\prime})\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})

In fact this is not a
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimal inference, but we write this one here for comparison to two of the new 8-variable inferences in the next subsection.
It can be checked valid and non-trivial by simply checking all cases, or by use of a solver.
We do not give an argument for
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence here, but such an argument is similar to the one we give for an 8-variable inference
Eq.

9
given in the next subsection.

Remark 13

(Deriving 3-2-pigeonhole using contraction)
.

It is known that, when we add to
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
structural rules that allow us to ‘duplicate’ and ‘erase’ propositional variables then the resulting (non-linear) system can derive every linear inference.
This is thanks to the
completeness
of the deep inference system
\mathsf{SKS}
for propositional logic and its cut-elimination theorem (see, e.g.,
[
Brü03b
]
).
With respect to
Eq.

8
above, we need only a single ‘duplication’ on
z
or
z^{\prime}
.
For instance, here is the corresponding derivation for
z
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\underline{(({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z})\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))}\land(y\lor y^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})\\
\to_{\mathsf{m}}&({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\lor w)\land({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor w^{\prime})\land(y\lor y^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})\\
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}&\underline{({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\lor w)\land(y\lor y^{\prime})}\land\underline{({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor w^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})}\land(z^{\prime}\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&(({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land y)\lor w\lor y^{\prime})\land({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor u\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime}))\land(z^{\prime}\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))\\
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}&\underline{(({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land y)\lor w\lor y^{\prime})\land(z^{\prime}\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))}\land({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor u\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&(({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land y)\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))\land(({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor u)\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land y)\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\lor(\underline{((x\land x^{\prime})\land({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\lor u))}\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land y)\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\lor(x\land{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z})\lor(x^{\prime}\land u)\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\\
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}&\underline{({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\land x)\lor({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}\land y)}\lor(u\land x^{\prime})\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\\
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{m}}&(({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}\lor{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z})\land(x\lor y))\lor(u\land x^{\prime})\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\end{array}

where we have underlined some redexes for clarity.
The purple
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}z}
and orange
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}z}
form what is known as a
contraction loop
in the deep inference literature, cf.
[
Das15
]
.

3.2.
The four minimal 8 variable
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independent linear inferences

Pre-empting
Section

5.2
, let us explicitly give the four minimal linear inferences found by our algorithm and justify their
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence and
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality, as a sort of sanity check for our implementation later.
As we will see, they turn out to be significant in their own right, which is why we take the time to consider them separately.

3.2.1.
Refinements of the 3-2-pigeonhole-principle

First let us consider an 8 variable linear inference that may be used to derive
Eq.

8
(identical to (
1
) from the introduction):

\displaystyle(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))

(9)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(z\land(x\lor y))\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land((w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime}))

Recalling the notion of ‘duality’ from
Section

2.1
, let us formally define the
dual
of a linear inference
\varphi\to\chi
to be the linear inference
\bar{\chi}\to\bar{\varphi}
, where
\bar{\varphi}
and
\bar{\chi}
are obtained from
\varphi
and
\chi
, respectively, by flipping all
\lor
s to
\land
s and vice-versa.
Considering linear inferences up to renaming of variables, we have:

{obs}

(
9
) is self-dual.

Indeed, the formula structure of the RHS is clearly the dual of that of the LHS, and the mapping from a variable in the LHS to the variable at the same position in the RHS is, in fact, an involution. I.e.,
u
is mapped to itself;
v
is mapped to
y
which in turn is mapped to
v
;
v^{\prime}
is mapped to
w
which is in turn mapped to
v^{\prime}
;
x
is mapped to
y^{\prime}
which in turn is mapped to
x
; and
z
is mapped to itself.
Validity may be routinely checked by any solver, but we give a case analyis of assignments in
Section

A.1
.

We may also establish
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence and
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality by checking all applications of
\mathsf{s}
or
\mathsf{m}
to the LHS (note that we do not need to check the RHS, by
Section

3.2.1
above).
This analysis is given explicitly in
Section

A.4
.

We also found the following inference (identical to
Eq.

2
):

\displaystyle(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))

(10)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle((z\lor(w\land x))\land(x^{\prime}\lor y))\lor((w^{\prime}\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})

At first glance this looks very similar to (
9
), however it turns out that it is not isomorphic to it, and neither is derivable from the other. Like (
9
), it is also self dual. Validity of this inference is checked in
Section

A.2
, and its
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality is checked in
Section

A.5
(similarly to the (
9
), by duality we only need to check applications of
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{m}
from the LHS).

{exa}

[Deriving 3-2-pigeonhole using (
9
) and (
10
)]
As we suggested, the linear inference
Eq.

8
is in fact derivable from each of
Eq.

9
and
Eq.

10
above
(modulo
\{\mathsf{s}\}
).
Here is the derivation in
\{\mathsf{s},\eqref{eq:php32-derived-inf-repeated}\}
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land(y\lor y^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})\\
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}&(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land\underline{(y\lor y^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}&\underline{(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\to_{\eqref{eq:php32-derived-inf-repeated}}&((z\land(x\lor y))\lor(\underline{(w\lor y^{\prime})\land((w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime}))})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}&((z\land(x\lor y))\lor(w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&((z\land(x\lor y))\lor(\underline{w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&(z\land(x\lor y))\lor(u\land x^{\prime})\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\end{array}

where we have underlined certain redexes.
In
\{\mathsf{s},\eqref{eq:unknown-inference-8-repeated}\}
we have the derivation:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land(y\lor y^{\prime})\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})\\
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}&(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land\underline{(y\lor y^{\prime})\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor z^{\prime})}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}&\underline{(z\lor(w\land w^{\prime}))\land((x\land x^{\prime})\lor((y\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\to_{\eqref{eq:unknown-inference-8-repeated}}&(\underline{((z\lor(w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime}))\land(x\lor y))}\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{s}}&((z\land(x\lor y))\lor(w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))\land(u\lor u^{\prime})\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&((z\land(x\lor y))\lor(\underline{w^{\prime}\land x^{\prime}\land(u\lor u^{\prime})})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime}))\\
\overset{*}{\rightsquigarrow}_{\mathsf{s}}&(z\land(x\lor y))\lor(u\land x^{\prime})\lor(w^{\prime}\land u^{\prime})\lor((w\lor y^{\prime})\land z^{\prime})\end{array}

Again we have underlined some redexes and, in the instance of
Eq.

10
, we have applied the permutation
\{(w,w^{\prime}),(x,x^{\prime})\}
.
Note that these two derivations are almost identical, differing only in the switch application immediately after the instance of
Eq.

9
or
Eq.

10
respectively.

3.2.2.
Counterexamples to a conjecture of Das and Straßburger

Finally, our search algorithm found two completely new linear inferences, which were dual to eachother. One of them
(identical to (
3
) from the introduction) is:

\displaystyle((w\land w^{\prime})\lor(x\land x^{\prime}))\land((y\land y^{\prime})\lor(z\land z^{\prime}))

(11)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(w\land y)\lor((x\lor(w^{\prime}\land z^{\prime}))\land((x^{\prime}\land y^{\prime})\lor z))

with the other given by dualising the above inference.
Again, validity is routine, but a case analysis is given in
Section

A.3
.
We may establish
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-independence and
\{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{m}\}
-minimality again by checking all possible rule applications.
This analysis is given in
Section

A.6
.

This new inference exhibits a rather interesting property, which we shall frame in terms of the following notion, since it will be used in the next section:

{defi}

Let
\varphi
be a linear formula on a variable set
\mathcal{V}
. For distinct
x,y\in\mathcal{V}
, the
least common connective
(lcc) of
x
and
y
in
\varphi
is the connective
\lor
or
\land
at the root of the smallest subformula of
\varphi
containing both
x
and
y
.

Note that, in the inference (
11
) above, the lcc of
w^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
changes from
\lor
to
\land
, but the lcc of
y
and
y^{\prime}
changes from
\land
to
\lor
.
No such example of a minimal linear inference exhibiting both of these properties was known before; switch, medial and all of the linear inferences of this section either preserve
\lor
-lccs or preserve
\land
-lccs.
In fact, Das and Straßburger showed that any valid linear inference preserving
\land
-lccs is already derivable by medial
[
DS16
, Theorem 7.5]
.
Furthermore they made the following conjecture:

Conjecture 7.9 from
[
DS16
]
, rephrased.

Each logically minimal nontrivial linear inference preserves either
\land
-lccs or
\lor
-lccs.

Naturally, (
11
) and
\overline{\eqref{eq:counterexample-inference-repeated}}
are counterexamples to that:

Theorem 14
.

Conjecture 7.9 from
[
DS16
]
is false.

We shall see several further counterexamples over 9 variables later in
Section

7
, where we also discuss possible consequences and trends arising from all these new inferences.

4.
A graph-theoretic presentation of linear inferences

In this section we consider graphical representations of formulas that render our search algorithm in
Section

5
more efficient.
The content of this section is based on Section 4 from the preliminary version,
[
DR21
]
, with the addition of further examples, figures and narrative.

A significant cause of algorithmic complexity when searching for linear inferences is the multitude of formulae equivalent modulo associativity and commutativity (
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
). For example, for 7 variables, there are
42577920
formulae (ignoring units), yet only
78416
equivalence classes.
Under
4
it would be ideal if we could deal with
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
-equivalence classes directly, realising logical and syntactic notions on them in a natural way.
This is precisely what is accomplished by the graph-theoretic notion of a
relation web
, cf.
[
Gug07
,
Str07
,
DS15
,
DS16
]
.

Throughout this section we work only with constant-free negation-free linear formulae, cf.
7
.
Recall the notion of
least common connective
(lcc) from
Section

3.2.2
.

{defi}

Let
\varphi
be a linear formula on a variable set
\mathcal{V}
.
The
relation web
(or simply
web
) of
\varphi
, written
\mathcal{W}(\varphi)
, is a simple undirected graph with:

•

The set of nodes of
\mathcal{W}(\varphi)
is just
\mathcal{V}
, i.e. the variables occurring in
\varphi
.

•

For
x,y\in\mathcal{V}
, there is an edge between
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{W}(\varphi)
if the lcc of
x
and
y
in
\varphi
is
\land
.

When we draw graphs, we will draw a solid red line
x
y
if there is an edge between
x
and
y
, and a green dotted line
x
y
otherwise.

{exa}

Let
\varphi
be the linear formula
w\land(x\land(y\lor z))
.
\mathcal{W}(\varphi)
is the following graph:

w
x
y
z

Note that linear formulae equivalent up to associativity and commutativity have the same relation web, since
\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}
does not affect the lccs.
For instance, if
\psi=(w\land x)\land(z\lor y)
, then
\mathcal{W}(\psi)
is still just the relation web above.
In fact, we also have the converse:

Proposition 15

(E.g.,
[
DS16
]
, Proposition 3.5)
.

Given linear formulae
\varphi
and
\psi
,
\varphi\sim_{\mathsf{ac}}\psi
if and only if
\mathcal{W}(\varphi)=\mathcal{W}(\psi)
.

Thus relation webs are natural representations of equivalence classes of linear formulae modulo associativity and commutativity.

We can also realise our new 8-variable inferences from the previous section graphically:

{exa}

The inferences (
9
), (
10
) and (
11
) may be construed as graph rewrites on their webs, as given in
Fig.

1
.
The one for
\overline{\eqref{eq:counterexample-inference-repeated}}
is obtained from that of (
11
) by switching every edge to a non-edge and vice-versa, and exchanging the LHS and RHS.
Note, for (
11
), that the edge between
y^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}
changes from red to green, and several edges (e.g.
x^{\prime}
to
y
) change from green to red, exhibiting how this inference breaks Conjecture 7.9 from
[
DS16
]
.

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

\to

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

\to

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

\to

w
w^{\prime}
x
x^{\prime}
y
y^{\prime}
z
z^{\prime}

Figure 1.
Graphical versions of the 8-variable inferences (
9
), (
10
) and (
11
) respectively.

It is easy to see that the image of
\mathcal{W}
is just the
cographs
. A
cograph
is either a single node, or has the form
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{S}
or
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{S}
for cographs
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
.
Formally,
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{S}
has as nodes the disjoint union of the nodes of
\mathcal{R}
and the nodes of
\mathcal{S}
; edges within the
\mathcal{R}
component are inherited from
\mathcal{R}
and similarly for
\mathcal{S}
; there is also an edge between every node in
\mathcal{R}
and every node in
\mathcal{S}
.
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{S}
is defined similarly, but without the last clause.

A
cograph decomposition
of a cograph
\mathcal{R}
is just a definition tree according to these construction rules (its ‘cotree’), which we identify with a linear formula (whose web is
\mathcal{R}
) in the natural way, setting
\land
for a
node and
\lor
for a
node.
By
15
, the cograph decomposition of a cograph is unique up to associativity and commutativity of
\land
and
\lor
.

Cographs admit an elegant
local
characterisation by means of forbidden subgraphs:

{defi}

An
induced
subgraph of a graph
G
is one whose edges are just those of
G
restricted to some subset of the nodes.
A graph
G
is
P_{4}
-free
if none of its induced subgraphs are isomorphic to the following graph, called
P_{4}
:

w
x
y
z

Further define a
clique

P
of a graph
G
to be a subset of the nodes of
G
such that the induced subgraph on these nodes has all possible edges. Dually define a
stable set
to be a subset of the nodes whose induced subgraph contains no edges. A clique or stable set is
maximal
if any proper superset is no longer a clique or stable set respectively.

The following result is well-known since (at least) the ’70s, and has appeared numerous times in the literature (see also
[
Gug07
,
Str07
]
):

Proposition 16

(E.g.
[
CLB81
]
)
.

A graph is a cograph if and only if it is
P_{4}
-free.

Thus, relation webs are just the
P_{4}
-free graphs whose nodes are variables.
Note, in particular, that this characterisation gives us an easy way to check whether a graph is the web of some formula: just check every
4
-tuple of nodes for a
P_{4}
.

{exa}

P_{5}
and
C_{5}
are two examples of graphs which are not
P_{4}
free. They are the following:

P_{5}
:
u
w
x
y
z

C_{5}
:
u
w
x
y
z

In both these cases, the induced subgraph generated from
\{u,w,x,y\}
is
P_{4}
.

What is more, we may also verify several semantic properties of linear inferences, such as validity and triviality, directly at the level of relation webs:

Proposition 17

(Follows from Proposition 4.4 and Theorem 4.6 in
[
DS16
]
)
.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be linear formulae on the same set of variables with webs
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
respectively. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\varphi\to\psi
is a valid linear inference.

(2)

For every maximal clique
P
of
\mathcal{R}
, there is some
Q\subseteq P
such that
Q
is a maximal clique of
\mathcal{S}
.

(3)

For every maximal stable set
Q
of
\mathcal{S}
, there is some
P\subseteq Q
such that
P
is a maximal stable set of
\mathcal{R}
.

Note that for inferences between arbitrary graphs (i.e. graphs that are not
P_{4}
-free), conditions (
2
) and (
3
) above are not equivalent.
In fact, (3) holds for
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{S}
exactly when (2) holds for
\overline{\mathcal{S}}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is the dual of
\mathcal{R}
(inverting every edge).

Example 18

(
[
DS16
]
)
.

Recall the definition of
P_{5}
and
C_{5}
from
Section

4
. It can be checked that
P_{5}\to C_{5}
satisfies (
2
) but
C_{5}\to P_{5}
does not (as
\{u,z\}
is a maximal clique of
C_{5}
with no subset that is a maximal clique of
P_{5}
). Conversely
C_{5}\to P_{5}

does
satisfy (
3
) but
P_{5}\to C_{5}
does not (for example
\{x,z\}
is a maximal stable set of
C_{5}
but none of
\{x,z\}
is stable but not maximal in
P_{5}
as
\{u,x,z\}
is stable).

Because
\mathcal{W}(\overline{\varphi})=\overline{\mathcal{W}(\varphi)}
, we see that (
2
) and (
3
) coincide for
P_{4}
-free graphs (as
\varphi\to\chi
is a valid linear inference precisely when
\overline{\chi}\to\overline{\varphi}
is).
Such relationships are further discussed in
[
CDW20
]
.

{propC}

[
[
DS16
, Proposition 5.7]
]

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be linear formulae on the same variables with webs
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
respectively.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

\varphi\to\psi
is a linear inference that is trivial at
x
.

(2)

For every maximal clique
P
of
\mathcal{R}
, there is some
Q\subseteq P\setminus\{x\}
such that
Q
is a maximal clique of
\mathcal{S}
.

(3)

For every maximal stable set
Q
of
\mathcal{S}
there is some
P\subseteq Q\setminus\{x\}
such that
P
is a maximal stable set of
\mathcal{R}
.

Note that the criterion for triviality is a strict strengthening of that for validity, as we would expect.
Again note the duality between the second and third characterisations of triviality above.

{exa}

[Validity of switch and medial, triviality of mix]
The switch and medial inferences can be construed as the following graph rewrite rules on relation webs, respectively:

It is easy to see that the validity criterion of
17
holds for each of these rules.
For
\mathsf{s}
, the maximal cliques
\{x,y\}
and
\{x,z\}
in the LHS are mapped to
\{x,y\}
and
\{z\}
in the RHS respectively.
For
\mathsf{m}
, the maximal cliques
\{w,x\}
and
\{y,z\}
in the LHS are mapped to themselves in the RHS.

Now consider the trivial inference
x\land y\to x\lor y
, construed as the graph rewrite rule:

We can easily verify the criterion for triviality at
x
from
Section

4
since the only maximal clique on the LHS,
\{x,y\}
has
\{y\}\subseteq\{x,y\}\setminus\{x\}
as a maximal clique on the RHS.

{exa}

[Supermix]
A further interesting example is given by a class of inferences from
[
Das13
]
known as ‘supermix’. These have the following form:

a\land\bigg(\bigvee_{i<n}b_{i}\bigg)\to a\lor\bigg(\bigwedge_{i<n}b_{i}\bigg)

Each of these inferences is trivial, as long as
n>0
, and moreover they are trivial at each
b_{i}
‘at the same time’.
Note that, for
n>3
, they are examples of inferences that that have more
\land
symbols in the RHS than the LHS, and for
n>4
more edges in the RHS than the LHS.
For instance, considering
n=4
,

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
